It was a tenner, to be finickingly accurate about details, but Jimmy did not say so. He concluded, rightly, that the memory of the original five pounds which he had lent Lord Dreever at the Savoy Hotel had faded from the other’s mind.

“Don’t mention it,” he said.
===Chinese===
「那还用说，」勋爵大人说。「换了谁都会。」

「可是，斯宾塞，容我——」

「你把我这一生都搅苦了，」勋爵大人说，紧张地蹙起一副拜伦式的眉头。「你干的就是这个——搅苦了我整个见鬼的人生。我过的日子糟透了。我只得四处向朋友们伸手告贷，才能维持场面。怎么着，我还欠你五镑来着，对不对，皮特，老伙计？」

说句抠字眼的实话，那是十镑；但吉米没有点破。他断定——断定得不错——当初在萨伏伊大饭店借给德利弗勋爵的那五镑，早已从对方记忆里褪了色。

「不值一提，」他说。

===Original===
“But I do mention it,” protested his lordship shrilly. “It just proves what I say. If I had had a decent allowance it wouldn’t have happened. And you wouldn’t give me enough to set me going in the Diplomatic Service. That’s another thing. Why wouldn’t you do that?”

Sir Thomas pulled himself together.

“I hardly thought you qualified, my dear boy.”

His lordship did not actually foam at the mouth, but he looked as if he might do so at any moment. Excitement and the memory of his wrongs, lubricated, as it were, by the champagne he had consumed both at and after dinner, had produced in him a frame of mind far removed from the normal. His manners no longer had that repose which stamps the caste of Vere de Vere. He waved his hands.

“I know, I know!” he shouted. “I know you didn’t. You thought me a fearful fool. I tell you I’m sick of it. And always trying to make me marry money! Dashed humiliating! If she hadn’t been a jolly sensible girl you’d have spoiled Miss McEachern’s life as well as mine. You came very near it. I tell you, I’ve had enough of it. I’m in love! I’m in love with the rippingest girl in England. You’ve seen her, Pitt, old top. Isn’t she a ripper?”
===Chinese===
「可我偏要提，」勋爵大人尖声抗议道。「这恰恰证明了我方才的话。当初我要是有份像样的津贴，这种事根本不会发生。还有，我要进外交界，你不肯给我一笔够用的钱让我起步。这是另一桩。你为什么不肯？」

托马斯爵士振作起来。

「我总觉得你不大够格，我亲爱的孩子。」

勋爵大人倒没有真个口吐白沫，可那副模样，随时随刻都像要吐出来似的。亢奋加上旧怨的记忆，再经饭桌上和饭后灌下的香槟酒这么好比上了润滑油，在他心里酿出了一种远离常态的心境。他那一举手一投足，再也端不出那种标明维尔德维尔（Vere de Vere）贵族门第的安详气度了。他挥舞着双手。

「我知道，我知道！」他嚷道。「我知道你不肯。你当我是个十足的大傻瓜。我告诉你，我受够了。还老是一门心思要我娶个有钱的！真是丢人现眼！她要不是个顶明白事理的好姑娘，你就不光要毁了我，连麦克伊彻恩小姐的一生也要给毁了。你已经差一点儿就毁了。我告诉你，我受够了。我恋爱了！我爱上了全英国顶呱呱的好姑娘。你见过她的，皮特，老伙计。她是不是顶呱呱？」

===Original===
Jimmy stamped the absent lady with the seal of his approval.

“I tell you, if she’ll have me, I’m going to marry her.”

The dismay written on every inch of Sir Thomas’s countenance became intensified at these terrific words. Great as had been his contempt for the actual holder of the title considered simply as a young man, he had always been filled with a supreme respect for the Dreever name.

“But, Spencer,” he almost howled, “consider your position! You cannot﻿—”

“Can’t I, by Jove! if she’ll have me; and dash my position! What’s my position got to do with it? Katie’s the daughter of a general, if it comes to that. Her brother was at the House with me. If I had a penny to call my own I’d have asked her to marry me ages ago. Don’t you worry about my position!”
===Chinese===
吉米给这位不在场的姑娘盖上了赞许的大印。

「我告诉你，只要她肯要我，我就要娶她。」

这几句惊天动地的话一出，托马斯爵士满脸每一寸地方写着的惊惶又加深了一层。尽管单把这位头衔的现实持有人当个年轻人看，他向来是何等瞧不上眼，他心里对德里弗这个姓氏却始终怀着无上的敬意。

「可是，斯宾塞，」他几乎嚎叫起来，「想想你的地位！你不能——」

「我不能！凭老天爷，只要她肯要我；去他的地位！我的地位跟这有什么相干？再说了，凯蒂是位将军的女儿。她哥哥跟我在议院里还同过事呢。但凡我有一个子儿是属于自己的，我早就向她求过婚了。你就甭替我的地位操心啦！」

===Original===
Sir Thomas croaked feebly.

“Now, look here,” said his lordship, with determination. “Here’s the whole thing in a jolly old nutshell. If you want me to forget about this little flutter in fake diamonds of yours you’ve got to pull your socks up and start in to do things. You’ve got to get me attached to some Embassy, for a beginning. It won’t be difficult. There’s dozens of old boys in London who knew the governor when he was alive who will jump at the chance of doing me a good turn. I know I’m a bit of an ass in some ways, but that’s expected of you in the Diplomatic Service. They only want you to wear evening clothes as if you were used to them, and be a bit of a flier at dancing, and I can fill the bill all right as far as that goes. And you’ve got to give your jolly old blessing to Katie and me﻿—if she’ll have me. That’s about all I can think of for the moment. Now do we go? Are you on?”

“It’s preposterous,” began Sir Thomas.
===Chinese===
托马斯爵士有气无力地嘎了一声。

「好，听着，」勋爵大人拿定了主意说。「整个事情，一言以蔽之，是这么回事。你要我忘掉你那桩假钻石的小投机，就得打起精神来，撸起袖子干点实事。头一件，你得替我在哪个使馆里谋个随员的位置。这不难。伦敦有几十位老先生，先父在世的时候都认得，如今巴不得有个机会帮我一把。我知道自己在有些方面是有点儿蠢，可干外交这一行，要的就是这个。他们要你的，无非是穿上晚礼服要像穿惯了似的，舞再跳得漂亮点儿，这几样我全能对付下来。还有，你得把你那见鬼的老祝福赏给凯蒂和我——要是她肯要我的话。眼下我一时想得起来的，就这几点。怎么样，咱们下楼吧？这桩买卖你接不接？」

「这真是岂有此理，」托马斯爵士刚一开口。

===Original===
Lord Dreever gave the door handle a rattle. He stopped.

“It’s a holdup all right,” said Jimmy soothingly. “I don’t want to butt in on a family conclave, but my advice, if asked, would be to unbelt before the shooting begins. You’ve got something worse than a pipe pointing at you now. As regards my position in the business, don’t worry. My silence is thrown in gratis. Give me one loving smile and my lips are sealed.”

Sir Thomas turned on him.

“As for you﻿—” he cried.

“Never mind about Pitt,” said his lordship. “He’s a dashed good fellow, Pitt. I wish there were more like him. And he wasn’t pinching the stuff either. If you had only listened when he tried to tell you, you mightn’t be in such a frightful hole. He was putting the things back, as he said. I know all about it. Well, what’s the answer?”
===Chinese===
德利弗勋爵把门把摇得咔啦咔啦响。随后住了手。

「没错儿，这是一场地地道道的持械拦劫，」吉米抚慰道。「人家家庭会议，我不想横插一杠子；不过倘蒙垂询，我的忠告是：趁枪还没响，赶紧把腰带解下来。这会儿对着你的家伙，可比一支烟斗厉害。至于我在这个买卖里算个什么，别操心。我的沉默是白饶的。只消赏我一个亲爱的微笑，我马上守口如瓶。」

托马斯爵士冲他转过身来。

「至于你——」他嚷道。

「皮特你就甭管了，」勋爵大人说。「皮特是个顶好的家伙。这样的人再来几个才好。再说，那玩意儿也不是他偷的。当初他试着跟你分说，你但凡肯听上一句，也不至于落进这么吓人的坑里。他把东西放回原处去了，他怎么说他怎么做。这里头的底细我全知道。怎么样，给句痛快话吧？」

===Original===
For a moment Sir Thomas seemed on the point of refusal. But just as he was about to speak his lordship opened the door, and at the movement he collapsed again.

“I will!” he cried. “I will!”

“Good,” said his lordship, with satisfaction. “That’s a bargain. Coming downstairs, Pitt, old man? We shall be wanted on the stage in about half a minute.”
===Chinese===
有一霎时，托马斯爵士似乎就要一口回绝。可正当他要开口，勋爵大人把门拉开了；就这一下，他又瘫了下去。

「我答应！」他叫道。「我答应！」

「好，」勋爵大人满意地说。「一言为定。下楼去吗，皮特，老伙计？再有半分钟光景，台上就要用咱们了。」

===Original===
“As an antidote to stage fright,” said Jimmy, as they went along the corridor, “little discussions of that kind may be highly recommended. I shouldn’t mind betting that you feel fit for anything.”

“I feel like a two-year-old,” assented his lordship enthusiastically. “I’ve forgotten all my part, but I don’t care. I’ll just go on and talk to them.”

“That,” said Jimmy, “is the right spirit. Charteris will get heart disease, but it’s the right spirit. A little more of that sort of thing and amateur theatricals would be worth listening to. Step lively, Roscius; the stage waits.”
===Chinese===
「作为怯场的一味解药，」两人沿着走廊走的时候，吉米说，「方才那类小小的谈判，值得大力推荐。我敢打赌，这会儿你觉得自己干什么都成。」

「我觉得自己像匹两岁的小马驹，」勋爵大人兴冲冲地附和道。「台词我忘了个一干二净，可我不在乎。我就上台去跟他们聊。」

「这才对，」吉米说。「就是这股子精神。查特里斯要得心脏病的，可这的确是那股子精神。这种劲头再来上几回，业余演剧就值得人侧耳倾听了。迈开步子吧，罗斯基乌斯（Roscius，古罗马名优）——台上等着呢。」

## XXVIII Spennie’s Hour of Clear Vision / 斯宾尼的顿悟时刻

===Original===
Mr. McEachern sat in the billiard room smoking. He was alone. From where he sat he could hear distant strains of music. The more rigorous portion of the evening’s entertainment, the theatricals, was over, and the nobility and gentry, having done their duty by sitting through the performance, were now enjoying themselves in the ballroom. Everybody was happy. The play had been quite as successful as the usual amateur performance. The prompter had made himself a great favourite from the start, his series of duets with Spennie having been especially admired, and Jimmy, as became an old professional, had played his part with great finish and certainty of touch, though, like the bloodhounds in the performance of *Uncle Tom’s Cabin*, on tour, he had had poor support. But the audience bore no malice.

No collection of individuals is less vindictive than an audience at amateur theatricals. It was all over now. Charteris had literally gibbered in the presence of eyewitnesses at one point in the second act, when Spennie, by giving a wrong cue, had jerked the play abruptly into Act III (where his colleagues, dimly suspecting something wrong, but not knowing what, had kept it for some two minutes, to the mystification of the audience); but now even he had begun to forget. As he two-stepped down the room the lines of agony on his face were softened. He even smiled.

As for Spennie, the brilliance of his happy grin dazzled all beholders.

He was still wearing it when he invaded the solitude of Mr. McEachern. In every dance, however greatly he may be enjoying it, there comes a time when a man needs a meditative cigarette apart from the throng. It came to Spennie after the seventh item on the programme. The billiard room struck him as admirably suitable in every way. It was not likely to be used as a sitting out place, and it was near enough to the ballroom to enable him to hear when the music of item No. 9 should begin.

===Chinese===
麦克伊彻恩先生坐在台球室里抽烟。屋里只有他一个人。打他坐的地方，听得见远处飘来的乐声。今晚余兴节目里比较吃功夫的那一段——业余演剧——已经收场，贵族老爷和绅士淑女们端坐听完了整场演出，尽了自己的本分，这会儿正在舞厅里纵情作乐。人人都很快活。这出戏的成绩，跟寻常业余演出的一般水准恰是一模一样。提词人打一开场便大受爱戴，他与斯宾尼的一连串「二重唱」尤其为人称赏；吉米呢，不愧是老江湖出身，把他的角色演得精妙娴熟、手法笃定，只是——一如巡演版《汤姆叔叔的小屋》（Uncle Tom’s Cabin）里的那些猎犬——他得到的配合也少得可怜。好在观众并无恶意。

世上再没有哪一群人，比业余演剧的观众更不记仇的了。如今一切都已过去。第二幕演到某个节骨眼上，斯宾尼递错了一句接口，把戏冷不防拽进了第三幕（同台众伙伴隐隐约约觉出不对，又说不上哪儿不对，竟在那一幕里硬撑了约莫两分钟，把台下观众看得莫名其妙），查特里斯当着好几位目击证人的面，实实在在地急得语无伦次；可眼下连他也开始淡忘了。他一路踏着两步舞穿过舞池，脸上那几道痛苦的纹路已经柔和了下来。他甚至还露出了笑容。

至于斯宾尼，他咧着嘴的那副欢天喜地的笑，光彩照人，晃得看见的人个个睁不开眼。

他闯进麦克伊彻恩先生的清静独处之地时，那副笑还挂在脸上。无论一场舞跳得多尽兴，人总有那么一刻，需要离开人群，独自抽一支供沉思用的香烟。节目单演过第七项之后，这一刻降临到了斯宾尼身上。台球室在他看来处处合式：多半不会有人挑它当歇舞的清静角落，离舞厅又近，第九项的音乐一响，他准能听见。

===Original===
Mr. McEachern was glad to see him. In the turmoil following the theatricals he had been unable to get a word with any of the persons with whom he most wished to speak. He had been surprised that no announcement of the engagement had been made at the end of the performance. Spennie would be able to supply him with information as to when the announcement might be expected.

Spennie hesitated for an instant when he saw who was in the room. He was not overanxious for a tête-à-tête with Molly’s father just then; but reflecting that after all he, Spennie, was not to blame for any disappointment that might be troubling the other, he switched on his grin again and walked in.

“Came in for a smoke,” he explained, by way of opening the conversation. “Not dancing the next.”

===Chinese===
麦克伊彻恩先生见他来了很高兴。演剧散场后的一片乱哄哄里，他一直没能同他最想说话的任何人搭上一句。演出收场时竟没有宣布订婚，他心里正纳闷。斯宾尼准能向他提供情报：宣布大约要等到什么时候。

斯宾尼一眼看清屋里坐的是谁，迟疑了一瞬。这个当口，他并不怎么巴望同莫莉的父亲促膝密谈；不过转念一想，就算对方胸中正郁结着什么失望，反正也怪不到他斯宾尼头上，便重新拧亮了那副咧嘴笑，走了进去。

「进来抽口烟，」他开门见山地解释道。「下一支曲子我不跳。」

===Original===
“Come in, my boy, come in,” said Mr. McEachern. “I was waiting to see you.”

Spennie regretted his entrance. He had supposed that the other had heard the news of the breaking-off of the engagement. Evidently, from his manner, he had not. This was a nuisance.

He sat down and lit a cigarette, casting about the while for an innocuous topic of conversation.

“Like the show?” he inquired.

===Chinese===
「进来吧，我的孩子，进来，」麦克伊彻恩先生说。「我正等着见你呢。」

斯宾尼后悔跨进了这道门。他原以为对方已经听说了订婚告吹的消息。瞧这神气，显然还没有。这可麻烦了。

他坐下来，点上一支烟，一面搜索枯肠，想寻个不伤筋动骨的话头。

「戏还行吧？」他探问道。

===Original===
“Fine,” said Mr. McEachern. “By the way—”

Spennie groaned inwardly. He had forgotten that a determined man can change the conversation to any subject he pleases by means of those three words.

“By the way,” said Mr. McEachern, “I thought Sir Thomas—wasn’t your uncle intending to announce—?”

“Well, yes, he was,” said Spennie.

“Going to declare it during the dancing, maybe?”

“Well—er—no. The fact is, he’s not going to do it at all, don’t you know.” He inspected the red end of his cigarette closely. “As a matter of fact, it’s kind of broken off.”

===Chinese===
「好得很，」麦克伊彻恩先生说。「对了——」

斯宾尼在心里叫苦。他忘了，一个意志坚决的人，单凭这三个字，就能把话头拐到他喜欢的任何题目上去。

「对了，」麦克伊彻恩先生说。「我还当托马斯爵士——你叔叔不是打算宣布——？」

「嗯，是的，本来是打算的，」斯宾尼说。

「打算趁着跳舞的时候宣布，是吧？」

「这个——呃——不。实话说，他压根儿不打算宣布了，您知道。」他凑近端详那截烧红的烟头。「实不相瞒，这事儿算是吹了。」

===Original===
The other’s exclamation jarred on him. Rotten, having to talk about this sort of thing!

“Broken off?”

Spennie nodded.

“Miss McEachern thought it over, don’t you know,” he said, “and came to the conclusion that it wasn’t good enough.”

===Chinese===
对方那一声惊呼叫他听着刺耳。真糟心，非得谈这种事不可！

「吹了？」

斯宾尼点了点头。

「麦克伊彻恩小姐把这件事细细想过了，您知道，」他说，「末了得出个结论：这门亲事配不上。」

===Original===
Now that it was said he felt easier. It had merely been the awkwardness of having to touch on the thing that had troubled him. That his news might be a blow to McEachern did not cross his mind. He was a singularly modest youth, and though he realised vaguely that his title had a certain value in some people’s eyes, he could not understand anyone mourning over the loss of him as a son-in-law. Katie’s father, the old general, thought him a fool, and once, during an attack of gout, had said so.

Oblivious, therefore, to the storm raging a yard away from him, he smoked on with great contentment, till suddenly it struck him that, for a presumably devout lover, jilted that very night, he was displaying too little emotion. He debated swiftly within himself whether he should have a dash at manly grief, but came to the conclusion that it could not be done. Melancholy on this maddest, merriest day of all the glad New Year, the day on which he had utterly routed the powers of evil, as represented by Sir Thomas, was impossible.

“It wouldn’t have done, don’t you know,” he said. “We weren’t suited. What I mean to say is, I’m a bit of a dashed sort of silly ass in some ways, if you know what I mean. A girl like Miss McEachern couldn’t have been happy with me. She wants one of those capable, energetic fellers.”

===Chinese===
话既出口，他自在了。先前折磨他的，无非是不得不触及这桩事的那份尴尬。他这条消息没准儿是对麦克伊彻恩当头一棒——这层意思压根儿没进过他的脑子。他是个出奇谦逊的少年，虽然隐隐约约也知道自己的头衔在某些人眼里有些分量，却怎么也不能设想会有谁为失去他这么一个女婿而痛心疾首。凯蒂的父亲，那位老将军，就认定他是个傻瓜，有一回痛风发作，还当面把这话说出了口。

于是，一码之外风暴翻腾，他浑然不觉，只管心满意足地抽烟，直到猛然省悟：作为一个照说该情深意笃的恋人，而且是当夜刚刚被甩的恋人，自己流露的哀情未免太少了些。他心里飞快地掂量，要不要冒险试它一试，装出一副男子汉的悲怆，随即得出结论：办不到。在整个欢乐新年里数它最癫狂、最快活的这个日子上——就在他一举击溃以托马斯爵士为化身的邪恶势力的这一天——忧郁是断无可能的。

「这桩婚事原本就成不了，您知道，」他说。「我们不般配。我是说，在某些方面，我这个人有点儿该死的傻蛋味道，假如您明白我的意思。像麦克伊彻恩小姐这样的姑娘，跟着我决计幸福不了。她要的是那种有能耐、有干劲的家伙。」

===Original===
This struck him as a good beginning—modest but not grovelling. He continued, tapping quite a respectably deep vein of philosophy as he spoke.

“You see, dear old top—I mean sir—you see, it’s like this. As far as women are concerned, fellers are divided into two classes. There’s the masterful, capable Johnnies and the—er—the other sort. Now, I’m the other sort. My idea of the happy married life is to be—well, not exactly downtrodden, but—you know what I mean—kind of second fiddle. I want a wife”—his voice grew soft and dreamy—“who’ll pet me a good deal, don’t you know, stroke my hair a lot, and all that. I haven’t it in me to do the master-in-my-own-house business. For me the silent-devotion touch—sleepin’ on the mat outside her door, don’t you know, when she wasn’t feeling well, and bein’ found there in the morning, and being rather cosseted for my thoughtlessness. That’s the sort of idea. Hard to put it quite OK, but you know the sort of thing I mean. A feller’s got to realise his jolly old limitations if he wants to be happy though married; what? Now, suppose Miss McEachern was to marry me! Great Scot, she’d be bored to death in a week! Honest. She couldn’t help herself. She wants a chap with the same amount of go in him that she’s got.”

He lit another cigarette. He was feeling pleased with himself. Never before had ideas marshalled themselves in his mind in such long and well-ordered ranks. He felt that he could go on talking like this all night. He was getting brainier every minute. He remembered reading in some book somewhere of a girl (or chappie) who had had her (or his) “hour of clear vision.” This was precisely what had happened now. Whether it was owing to the excitement of what had taken place that night, or because he had been keeping up his thinking powers with excellent dry champagne, he did not know. All he knew was that he felt on top of his subject. He wished he had had a larger audience. “A girl like Miss McEachern,” he resumed, “doesn’t want any of the hair-stroking business. She’d simply laugh at a feller if he asked for it. She needs a chappie of the Get On or Get Out type—somebody in the six-cylinder class. And as a matter of fact, between ourselves, I rather think she’s found him.”

===Chinese===
他觉得这个开头不坏——谦逊，而又不至于摇尾乞怜。他继续往下说，一路开掘，居然凿中了一脉深得可观的哲理矿层。

「您瞧，亲爱的老兄——我是说，先生——您瞧，是这么回事。说到女人，家伙们分成两类。一类是威风八面、精明强干的狠角色，另一类是——呃——另外那种。我呢，就是另外那种。我心目中的美满婚姻，就是当个——呃，倒也不说全然给人踩在脚下——您明白我的意思——算是拉第二小提琴。我想要一位太太，」他的声音变得轻柔而缥缈，「她会把我疼了又疼，您知道，常常抚弄我的头发，诸如此类。当自己家里的老爷那份儿营生，我身上没有这个材料。我走的是无言深情那一手——她不舒坦的夜里，您知道，就睡在她门外的小地毯上，第二天一早叫人发现，还就凭我这份不假思索，被好生心疼一场。就是这么个意思。要说得十分周全有点难，可我说的是哪一路事儿，您明白。一家伙要想结了婚还过得快活，就得认清自己那点儿老掉牙的斤两；您说是不是？喏，设想麦克伊彻恩小姐嫁给了我！老天，不出一个礼拜她就得闷死！真的。她忍不住的。她要的是一个跟她一样浑身是劲的小伙子。」

他又点上一支烟。他颇为沾沾自喜。从来不曾有过的事：一个个念头在他脑子里排成这么绵长而井然有序的方阵。他觉得这话他能一口气说到天亮。他每过一分钟便机灵一分。他记起曾在哪本书里读到过，说一个姑娘（或者小伙子）有过她（或者他）的「顿悟时刻」（hour of clear vision）。此刻发生的正是这么回事。是出于当晚种种变故带来的兴奋呢，还是因为他一路拿上好的干香槟酒保养着自己的思维能力，他不得而知。他只知道，这个题目他已全然在握。只可惜听众寥寥。「像麦克伊彻恩小姐这样的姑娘，」他接着说道，「根本不稀罕抚弄头发那一套。哪家伙敢讨这个，她准笑掉大牙。她要的是『要么往上爬，要么滚出去』那一类型的小伙子——六气缸那一级的角色。说实在的，咱们私底下讲，我颇觉得她已经找着了。」

===Original===
“What?”

Mr. McEachern half rose from his chair. All his old fears had come surging back.

“What do you mean?”

“Fact,” said his lordship, nodding. “Mind you, I don’t know for certain. As the girl says in the song, I don’t know, but I guess. What I mean to say is, they seemed jolly friendly and all that—calling each other by their Christian names, and so on.”

“Who?”

“Pitt,” said his lordship. He was leaning back, blowing a smoke ring at the moment, so did not see the look on the other’s face and the sudden grip of his fingers on the arms of his chair. He went on with some enthusiasm.

===Chinese===
「什么？」

麦克伊彻恩先生从椅子里半支起身来。旧日种种忧惧，齐齐涌回心头。

「你这是什么意思？」

「千真万确，」勋爵大人点头说道。「不过您听着，我并不敢说十拿九稳。就像歌里那姑娘唱的：我不知道，可我猜得着。我是说，他们看着要好得不得了，诸如此类——彼此直呼教名，等等。」

「谁？」

「皮特，」勋爵大人说。他这会儿正仰靠在椅背上，朝天吐着一枚烟圈，因此没有瞧见对方脸上的神色，也没有瞧见他十指骤然扣紧了椅子的扶手。他带着几分热忱继续往下说。

===Original===
“Jimmy Pitt!” he said. “Now, there’s a feller. Full of oats to the brim, and fairly bursting with go and energy. A girl wouldn’t have a dull moment with a chap like that. You know,” he proceeded confidentially, “there’s a lot in this idea of affinities. Take my word for it, dear old—sir. There’s a girl up in London, for instance. Now, she and I hit it off most amazingly. There’s hardly a thing we don’t think alike about. For instance, ‘The Merry Widow’ didn’t make a bit of a hit with her; nor did it with me, yet look at the millions of people who raved about it. And neither of us like oysters. We’re affinities—that’s why. You see the same sort of thing all over the place. It’s a jolly queer business. Sometimes makes me believe in re—in-what’s-its-name—you know what I mean. All that in the poem, you know. How does it go? ‘When you were a tiddley-om-pom and I was a thingummajig.’ Dashed brainy bit of work. I was reading it only the other day. Well, what I mean to say is, it’s my belief that Jimmy Pitt and Miss McEachern are by way of being something in that line. Doesn’t it strike you that they are just the sort to get on together? You can see it with half an eye. You can’t help liking a feller like Jimmy Pitt. He’s a sport! I wish I could tell you some of the things he’s done, but I can’t, for reasons; but you can take it from me he’s a sport. You ought to cultivate him. You’d like him. … Oh, dash it! there’s the music! I must be off. Got to dance this one.”

He rose from his chair and dropped his cigarette into the ashtray.

“So long,” he said, with a friendly nod. “Wish I could stop, but it’s no go. That’s the last letup I shall have tonight.”

He went out, leaving Mr. McEachern seated in his chair, a prey to many and varied emotions.

===Chinese===
「吉米·皮特！」他说。「这才叫家伙。满肚皮燕麦一直满到边儿，干劲和精力涨得简直要迸裂。跟这么一个人在一块儿，姑娘绝没有发闷的时辰。您知道，」他推心置腹地接着讲，「『秉性相投』（affinities）这套道理，讲究深着呢。信我的话，亲爱的老——先生。比方说，伦敦有这么一位姑娘。我跟她投缘投得神了。几乎没有一件事我们想不到一块儿去。比如说，《风流寡妇》（The Merry Widow）没叫她倾倒半分，也没叫我倾倒半分，可瞧瞧几百万如痴如狂捧场的看客。再说，我们俩都不爱吃牡蛎。我们就是秉性相投——缘故全在这儿。这一类事情放眼皆是。这里头的门道透着古怪。有时候都叫我相信轮——轮什么来着——您明白我的意思。诗里写的就是那个，您知道。怎么念来着？『当你还是个叮铃咚嘭，我还是个什么名堂。』真是段妙极了的聪明文章。前两天我才刚拜读过。呃，我是想说，依我看哪，吉米·皮特和麦克伊彻恩小姐，多多少少就算那一路上的。您不觉得，他们正是合得来的那一对儿吗？半只眼睛就瞧得出来。像吉米·皮特这样的家伙，您想不喜欢都办不到。他够朋友！他干下的那些事，有几桩我倒真愿意讲给您听，可碍于种种缘故，讲不得；不过您可以放心信我：他够朋友。您该多跟他亲近亲近。您会喜欢他的。……哎呀，该死！音乐响了！我得赶紧走。这一支非跳不可。」

他从椅子里站起身，把香烟丢进烟灰缸。

「回头见，」他友好地点一点头，说道。「真想多待一会儿，可不成。这是我今晚最后一回喘气的机会了。」

他走了出去，留下麦克伊彻恩先生枯坐在椅子里，听凭种种纷纭的情绪肆意啃啮。

## XXIX The Last Round / 最后一回合

===Original===
He had only been gone a few minutes when Mr. McEachern’s meditations were again interrupted. This time the visitor was a stranger to him—a dark-faced, clean-shaven man. He did not wear evening clothes, so could not be one of the guests; and Mr. McEachern could not place him immediately. Then he remembered. He had seen him in Sir Thomas Blunt’s dressing room. This was Sir Thomas’s valet.

“Might I have a word with you, sir?”

“What is it?” asked McEachern, staring heavily. His mind had not recovered from the effect of Lord Dreever’s philosophical remarks. There was something of a cloud on his brain. To judge from his lordship’s words, things had been happening behind his back; and the idea of Molly deceiving him was too strange to be assimilated in an instant. He looked at the valet dully.

“What is it?” he asked again.

===Chinese===
他前脚刚走没有几分钟，麦克伊彻恩先生的沉思便又被打断了。这回来访的是个他陌生的人——一张黑脸膛、刮得干干净净的脸。来客没有穿晚礼服，因此不会是宾客中的一员；麦克伊彻恩先生一时也认不出他是谁。随后他想起来了。他在托马斯·布伦特爵士的更衣室里见过此人。这是爵士老爷的贴身男仆。

「可以跟您说句话吗，先生？」

「什么事？」麦克伊彻恩问，重重地瞪着眼。他的脑子还没有从德利弗勋爵那一番哲理高论的效果里缓过来，脑中雾蒙蒙的一片。照勋爵大人的话听来，事情已经背着他在悄悄进行了；而莫莉会瞒着他这个念头，实在太古怪，一时半会儿咽不下去。他呆呆地望着那个男仆。

「什么事？」他又问了一遍。

===Original===
“I must apologise for intruding, but I thought it best to approach you before making my report to Sir Thomas.”

“Your report?”

“I am employed by a private inquiry agency.”

“What?”

“Yes, sir—Wragge’s. You may have heard of us, in Holborn Bars; very old established, divorce a speciality. You will have seen the advertisements. Sir Thomas wrote asking for a man, and the governor sent me down. I have been with the house some years. My job, I gathered, was to keep my eyes open generally. Sir Thomas, it seemed, had no suspicions of any definite person. I was to be on the spot just in case, in a manner of speaking. And it’s precious lucky I was, or her ladyship’s jewels would have been gone. I’ve done a fair cop this very night.”

===Chinese===
「冒昧打扰，还望包涵；只是我想，在向托马斯爵士呈交报告之前，最好先来见您。」

「你的报告？」

「我受雇于一家私家侦探社。」

「什么？」

「是，先生——Wragge 私家侦探社。您也许听说过我们，在霍尔博恩巴尔斯（Holborn Bars）；老字号，专办离婚。您总见过我们的广告。托马斯爵士写信要一个人，东家便把我派了下来。我在这个行里干了有些年头了。据我领会，我的差事总的来说就是把眼睛放亮些。托马斯爵士似乎并没有疑心哪个特定的人。可以说，我是守在现场以防万一的。这一守可真是万幸，要不然，夫人的珠宝早就没了。今儿晚上，我就干了一票干净利落的活儿。」

===Original===
He paused, and eyed the ex-policeman keenly. McEachern was obviously excited. Could Jimmy have made an attempt on the jewels during the dance? Or Spike?

“Say,” he said, “was it a red headed—?”

The detective was watching him with a curious smile.

“No, he wasn’t red headed. You seem interested, sir. I thought you would be. I will tell you all about it. I had had my suspicions of this party ever since he arrived. And I may say that it struck me at the time that there was something mighty fishy about the way he got into the castle.”

===Chinese===
他顿了一顿，锐利地打量这位当过警察的人。麦克伊彻恩显然激动了。莫非是吉米趁着跳舞的当口对珠宝下了手？还是斯派克？

「喂，」他说，「是个红头发的——？」

那侦探带着一丝古怪的微笑望着他。

「不，他不是红头发。您像是挺关心，先生。我就料到您会关心。我把事情原原本本讲给您听。打这位仁兄一到，我就疑上他了。我还可以说，当时我就觉得，他混进城堡来的路数蹊跷得厉害。」

===Original===
McEachern started. So he had not been the only one to suspect Jimmy’s motives in attaching himself to Lord Dreever.

“Go on,” he said.

“I suspected that there was some game on, and it struck me that this would be the day for the attempt, the house being upside down, in a manner of speaking, on account of the theatricals. And I was right. I kept near those jewels on and off all day, and presently, just as I had thought, along comes this fellow. He’d hardly got to the door when I was on him.”

“Good boy! You’re no rube.”

“We fought for a while, but, being a bit to the good in strength, and knowing something about the game, I had the irons on him pretty quick, and took him off and locked him in the cellar. That’s how it was, sir.”

===Chinese===
麦克伊彻恩吃了一惊。这么说，疑心吉米巴结德利弗勋爵别有图谋的，并非只有他一个。

「说下去，」他说。

「我疑心有什么勾当在暗中策划，又觉得下手的日子就该是今天，因为那业余演剧的缘故，府上可以说乱作了一团。我料得不错。我一整天断断续续守在那串珠宝近旁，果然，不出我所料，这位老兄来了。他还没走到门口，我已经扑到了他身上。」

「好小子！你可不是乡下土包子。」

「我们扭打了一阵子，不过，气力上我略占上风，又懂几分这行的门道，很快就给他铐上了铁镯子，把他带下去锁进了地窖。事情就是这样，先生。」

===Original===
Mr. McEachern’s relief was overwhelming. If Lord Dreever’s statement was correct, and Jimmy had really succeeded in winning Molly’s affection, this would be indeed a rescue at the eleventh hour. It was with a Nunc Dimittis air he felt for his cigar case and extended it towards the detective. A cigar from his own private case was with him a mark of the supremest favour and good will—a sort of accolade which he bestowed only upon the really meritorious few.

Usually it was received with becoming deference, but on this occasion there was a somewhat startling deviation from routine, for, just as he was opening the case, something cold and hard pressed against each of his wrists; there was a snap and a click, and looking up, dazed, he saw that the detective had sprung back, and was contemplating him with a grim smile over the barrel of an ugly-looking little revolver.

===Chinese===
麦克伊彻恩先生如释重负，感慨万千。倘若德利弗勋爵的话属实，吉米果真赢得了莫莉的芳心，这可真是最后关头的搭救。他带着一副念着《西面颂》（Nunc Dimittis）的功德圆满神情，伸手去摸自己的雪茄烟盒，朝侦探递过去。从他私人烟盒里敬出一支雪茄，在他这儿是最高的恩宠与善意的表征——一种只有真正有功之少数人才能领受的册封殊荣。

通常，这份殊荣总是由对方以得体的恭谨领受的；可这一回，常规的轨道上出现了颇为惊人的偏离：他正打开烟盒的当口，两只手腕上各贴上了一样又冷又硬的东西；啪的一声脆响，他晕晕乎乎抬起头来，只见那侦探已经纵身跳开，正隔着一支面目可憎的小左轮的枪管，狞笑着端详他。

===Original===
Guilty or innocent, the first thing a man does, when he finds handcuffs on his wrists, is to try to get them off. The action is automatic. Mr. McEachern strained at the steel chain till the veins stood out on his forehead. His great body shook with rage.

The detective eyed his efforts with some satisfaction. The picture presented by the other, as he heaved and tugged, was that of a guilty man trapped.

“It’s no good, my friend,” he said.

His voice brought McEachern back to his senses. In the first shock of the thing the primitive man in him had led him beyond the confines of self-restraint. He had simply struggled unthinkingly. Now he came to himself again.

He shook his manacled hands furiously.

===Chinese===
不管有罪没罪，一个人发觉手腕上铐了手铐，头一件事就是想法子把它弄掉。这是本能的动作。麦克伊彻恩先生拽那副钢链，直拽得额头青筋暴起。他魁伟的身躯气得直抖。

那侦探颇为满意地欣赏着他的努力。对方起起伏伏又拉又扯的那副模样，活脱是一幅赃犯落网的图画。

「白费力气的，我的朋友，」他说。

这声音把麦克伊彻恩唤回到神智上来。最初那一震惊里，他身上的原始人拖着他冲出了自制力的疆界。他只是不假思索地挣扎。这会儿他才醒过神来。

他狂怒地甩动那双戴着镣铐的手。

===Original===
“What does this mean?” he shouted. “What the—”

“Less noise,” said the detective sharply. “Get back!” he snapped, as the other took a step forward.

“Do you know who I am?” thundered McEachern.

“No,” said the detective. “And that’s just why you’re wearing those bracelets. Come, now, don’t be a fool, the game’s up; can’t you see that?”

McEachern leaned helplessly against the billiard table. He felt weak. Everything was unreal. Had he gone mad? he wondered.

===Chinese===
「这是什么意思？」他吼道。「搞什么——」

「少出声，」侦探尖声说。「退回去！」对方往前跨了一步，他断喝一声。

「你知道我是谁吗？」麦克伊彻恩雷霆般地喝道。

「不知道，」侦探说。「而这正是您戴着那对镯子的缘故。得了，别犯傻了，把戏已经唱完了，这还看不出来吗？」

麦克伊彻恩无可奈何地靠在台球桌上。他只觉得浑身发软。一切都是虚的。他疑心自己是不是疯了。

===Original===
“That’s right,” said the detective—“stay there. You can’t do any harm there. It was a pretty little game, I’ll admit. You worked it well—meeting your old friend from New York and all, and having him invited to the castle. Very pretty. New York, indeed! Seen about as much of New York as I have of Timbuktu. I saw through him.”

Some inkling of the truth began to penetrate McEachern’s consciousness. He had become so obsessed with the idea that, as the captive was not Spike, it must be Jimmy, that the possibility of Mr. Galer being the subject of discussion only dawned upon him now.

“What do you mean?” he cried. “Who is it that you have arrested?”

“Blest if I know. You can tell me that, I should think, seeing he’s an old Timbuktu friend of yours. Galer’s the name he goes by here.”

“Galer!”

===Chinese===
「这就对了，」侦探说——「待在那儿。待在那儿你使不了坏。我不否认，这是挺漂亮的一场小把戏。您导演得很出色——同纽约来的老朋友巧遇啦，又把他请进城堡来啦。妙极了。还纽约呢！他见过的纽约，跟我见过的廷巴克图（Timbuktu）差不多。我把他看穿了。」

一点真相的端倪开始渗进麦克伊彻恩的意识。他一心认定，那俘虏既不是斯派克，就必定是吉米，因此直到此刻才想到，所谈论的可能是盖勒先生。

「你这是什么意思？」他嚷道。「你抓的到底是谁？」

「天晓得。这个您该能告诉我吧，谁让他是您廷巴克图的老交情呢。他在这儿报的名字叫盖勒。」

「盖勒！」

===Original===
“That’s the man. And do you know what he had the impudence, the gall, to tell me? That he was in my own line of business. A detective! He said you had sent for him to come here.”

He laughed amusedly at the recollection.

“And so he is, you fool. So I did.”

“Oh, you did, did you? And what business had you bringing detectives into other people’s houses?”

Mr. McEachern started to answer, but checked himself. Never before had he appreciated to the full the depth and truth of the proverb relating to the frying pan and the fire. To clear himself he must mention his suspicions of Jimmy, and also his reasons for those suspicions. And to do that would mean revealing his past. It was Scylla and Charybdis.

===Chinese===
「就是他。您可知道他有多大脸面、多大狗胆，跟我说了什么？他说他跟我干的是同一行。侦探！他还说，是您写信把他请到这儿来的。」

想起这桩事，他饶有兴致地笑了。

「他确实是侦探，你这傻瓜。也确实是我请的他。」

「哦，是您请的，是吗？那您凭什么把侦探弄进别人的宅子？」

麦克伊彻恩先生刚要答话，又缩住了。他才算头一回充分领略到「才出煎锅，又入火坑」那句谚语是何等深刻、何等真切。要洗清自己，他就得提起自己对吉米的疑心，还得说出起疑的缘故。而这样做，就意味着揭自己的老底。真是前有锡拉岩礁，后有卡律布狄斯大漩涡，进退都是绝路。

===Original===
A drop of perspiration trickled down his temple.

“What’s the good?” said the detective. “Mighty ingenious idea, that, only you hadn’t allowed for there being a real detective in the house. It was that chap pitching me that yarn that made me suspicious of you. I put two and two together. ‘Partners,’ I said to myself. I’d heard all about you, scraping acquaintance with Sir Thomas and all. Mighty ingenious. You become the old family friend, and then you let in your pal. He gets the stuff and hands it over to you. Nobody dreams of suspecting you, and there you are. Honestly, now, wasn’t that the game?”

“It’s all a mistake—” McEachern was beginning, when the door handle turned.

The detective looked over his shoulder. McEachern glared dumbly. This was the crowning blow, that there should be spectators of his predicament.

===Chinese===
一滴汗珠从他太阳穴上滚了下来。

「何必呢，」侦探说。「这主意实在高明，只可惜您没算到府上还住着一位真侦探。就是那小子对我编的那套鬼话，叫我疑上了您。我把线索一拼，心里就有了数。『同伙』，我对自己说。您的事我全听说了，同托马斯爵士攀交情啦，等等。高明实在高明。您先当上这家的老朋友，再把您的伙伴放进门。他得手，东西交到您手里。谁也不会想到疑心您，这就大功告成了。凭良心说，是不是这么一场把戏？」

「这全是误会——」麦克伊彻恩刚起了个头，门把手转动了。

那侦探回头望了一眼。麦克伊彻恩呆呆地瞪着眼。祸不单行，他这般狼狈的境况，居然还有了观众。

===Original===
Jimmy strolled into the room.

“Dreever told me you were in here,” he said to McEachern. “Can you spare me a—— Halloa!”

The detective had pocketed his revolver at the first sound of the handle—to be discreet was one of the chief articles in the creed of the young men from Wragge’s Detective Agency—but handcuffs are not easily concealed. Jimmy stood staring in amazement at McEachern’s wrists.

“Some sort of a round game?” he inquired with interest.

===Chinese===
吉米踱进了房间。

「德利弗告诉我你在这儿，」他对麦克伊彻恩说。「能不能借一步——哎呀！」

门把手一响，那侦探就把左轮手枪揣回了口袋——谨慎行事，是 Wragge 私家侦探社青年子弟信条里的首要条款之一——可是手铐这东西，想藏可不容易。吉米站在那儿，惊愕地盯着麦克伊彻恩的手腕。

「是在玩什么牌局吗？」他饶有兴致地问。

===Original===
The detective became confidential.

“It’s this way, Mr. Pitt. There’s been some pretty deep work going on here. There’s a regular gang of burglars in the place. This chap here’s one of them.”

“What, Mr. McEachern?”

“That’s what he calls himself.”

It was all Jimmy could do to keep himself from asking Mr. McEachern whether he attributed his downfall to drink. He contented himself with a sorrowful shake of the head at the fermenting captive. Then he took up the part of counsel for the defence.

“I don’t believe it,” he said. “What makes you think so?”

“Why, this afternoon I caught this man’s pal—the fellow that calls himself Galer—”

“I know the man,” said Jimmy. “He’s a detective really. Mr. McEachern brought him down here.”

The sleuth’s jaw dropped limply, as if he had received a blow.

“What?” he said, in a feeble voice.

===Chinese===
侦探摆出了推心置腹的架势。

「事情是这样的，皮特先生。这座宅子里近来进行着相当深沉的勾当。此地有一伙如假包换的窃贼帮。这位老兄就是其中一员。」

「什么，麦克伊彻恩先生？」

「他是这么自称的。」

吉米费了九牛二虎之力，才忍住没问麦克伊彻恩先生：他可把自己的栽跟头归咎于贪杯？他满足于朝这位怒气发酵中的俘虏忧伤地摇一摇头。随后，他便担当起了辩护律师的角色。

「我不信，」他说。「你凭什么这么想？」

「嘿，今天下午我逮住了这人的同伙——那个自称盖勒的家伙——」

「这人我认识，」吉米说。「他其实是个侦探。是麦克伊彻恩先生把他带来的。」

那名猎手的下巴软塌塌地垂了下去，仿佛挨了一记闷拳。

「什么？」他有气无力地说。

===Original===
“Didn’t I tell you—” began Mr. McEachern; but the sleuth was occupied with Jimmy. That sickening premonition of disaster was beginning to steal over him. Dimly he began to perceive that he had blundered.

“Yes,” said Jimmy. “Why, I can’t say; but Mr. McEachern was afraid someone might try to steal Lady Julia Blunt’s rope of diamonds, so he wrote to London for this man Galer. It was officious, perhaps, but not criminal. I doubt if, legally, you could handcuff a man for a thing like that. What have you done with good Mr. Galer?”

“I’ve locked him in the coal cellar,” said the detective dismally. The thought of the interview in prospect with the human bloodhound he had so mishandled was not exhilarating.

“Locked him in the cellar, did you?” said Jimmy. “Well, well, I dare say he’s very happy there. He’s probably busy detecting black beetles. Still, perhaps you had better go and let him out. Possibly if you were to apologise to him—Eh? Just as you think—I only suggest. If you want somebody to vouch for Mr. McEachern’s non-burglariousness, I can do it. He is a gentleman of private means, and we knew each other out in New York.”

===Chinese===
「我不是告诉过你——」麦克伊彻恩先生刚开口；可那猎手满心只有吉米。大祸临头的那种令人作呕的预感，开始悄悄爬上他的心头。他隐隐约约地察觉，自己捅了娄子。

「是这么回事，」吉米说。「缘故嘛，我说不上来；可麦克伊彻恩先生唯恐有人打朱莉娅·布伦特夫人那串钻石项链的主意，便写信到伦敦，请来了这位盖勒先生。这或许是好管闲事，却谈不上犯法。照法律讲，为这么一桩事就铐人，我怀疑行不行得通。您把好心的盖勒先生怎么处置了？」

「我把他锁在煤窖里了，」侦探哭丧着脸说。想到即将同那条被他怠慢至此的人间血猎犬当面相见，心里实在舒畅不起来。

「把他锁进地窖了，是吗？」吉米说。「哎呀呀，我猜他在那儿快活得很呢。他八成正忙着侦查黑甲虫。话虽如此，您最好还是去放他出来。要是您肯向他赔个不是——嗯？悉听尊便——我只是提个建议。您要是需要人担保麦克伊彻恩先生并无梁上君子的行径，我可以担保。他是位坐拥私产的体面绅士，我们当年在纽约就认识。」

===Original===
“I never thought—”

“That,” said Jimmy, with sympathetic friendliness, “is the cardinal mistake you detectives make. You never do think.”

“It never occurred to me—”

He took the key of the handcuffs from his pocket and toyed with it. Mr. McEachern emitted a low growl. It was enough.

“If you wouldn’t mind, Mr. Pitt,” said the detective obsequiously. He thrust the key into Jimmy’s hands and fled. Jimmy unlocked the handcuffs. Mr. McEachern rubbed his wrists.

===Chinese===
「我根本没想到——」

「这个嘛，」吉米带着体贴的友善说，「就是你们这些侦探的头号通病。你们压根儿从来不动脑筋。」

「我从来没想到这一层——」

他从口袋里掏出手铐的钥匙，拿在指间把玩。麦克伊彻恩先生喉咙里滚出一声低吼。这就够了。

「请您费心收下吧，皮特先生，」侦探谄媚地说。他把钥匙塞进吉米手里，一溜烟逃了。吉米打开了手铐。麦克伊彻恩先生揉着手腕。

===Original===
“Ingenious little things,” said Jimmy.

“I’m much obliged to you,” growled Mr. McEachern, without looking up.

“Not at all—a pleasure. This circumstantial evidence business is the devil, isn’t it? I knew a man who broke into a house in New York to win a bet, and to this day the owner of the house thinks him a professional burglar.”

“What’s that?” said Mr. McEachern sharply.

===Chinese===
「真是精巧的小玩意儿，」吉米说。

「承你的情，」麦克伊彻恩先生闷声闷气地说，头也不抬。

「不值一提——乐意之至。这间接证据的玩意儿真是坑人，是不是？我认识一个人，在纽约为了赢一个赌，闯进了人家一所宅子；直到今天，那宅子的主人还当他是个职业窃贼。」

「你说什么？」麦克伊彻恩先生厉声问。

===Original===
“Why do I say ‘a man’? Why am I so elusive and mysterious? You’re quite right. It sounds more dramatic; but, after all, what you want is facts. Very well. I broke into your house that night to win a bet. That’s the limpid truth.”

McEachern was staring at him. Jimmy proceeded.

“You are just about to ask—what was Spike Mullins doing with me? Well, Spike had broken into my flat an hour before, and I took him along with me as a sort of guide, philosopher, and friend.”

“Spike Mullins said you were a burglar from England.”

===Chinese===
「我为什么说『一个人』？我为什么这么遮遮掩掩、神神秘秘？您说得对。这样讲是更有戏剧味；可说到底，您要的是事实。那好。那天晚上，我闯进您的宅子，是为了赢一个赌。这是清亮见底的实话。」

麦克伊彻恩直勾勾地瞪着他。吉米接着往下说。

「您正要问——斯派克·马林斯跟着我凑的哪门子热闹？是这样的，一个钟头之前，斯派克撬门闯进了我的公寓，我便带上他，让他充当我那向导、哲人与益友。」

「斯派克·马林斯说，您是个从英国来的窃贼。」

===Original===
“I’m afraid I rather led him to think so. I had been to see the opening performance of a burglar-play called *Love, the Cracksman* that night, and I worked off on Spike some severely technical information I had received from a pal of mine who played lead in the show. I told you when I came in that I had been talking to Lord Dreever. Well, what he was saying to me was that he had met this very actor-man, a fellow called Mifflin—Arthur Mifflin—in London just before he met me. He’s in London now, rehearsing for a show that’s come over from America. You see the importance of this item? It means that if you doubt my story all you need to do is to find Mifflin—I forget what theatre his play is coming on at, but you could find out in a second—and ask him to corroborate. Are you satisfied?”

McEachern did not answer. An hour before he would have fought to the last ditch for his belief in Jimmy’s crookedness, but the events of the last ten minutes had shaken him. He felt something of a reaction in Jimmy’s favour.

“Look here, Mr. McEachern,” said Jimmy, “I wish you would listen quietly to me for a minute or two. There’s really no reason on earth why we should be at one another’s throats in this way. We might just as well be friends. Let’s shake hands and call the fight off. I suppose you know why I came here to see you?” McEachern did not speak.

===Chinese===
「恐怕是我有意让他这么以为的。那天晚上，我刚看完一出叫《窃贼之恋》（Love, the Cracksman）的窃贼戏的首演，又把一位朋友传授给我的那些十足内行的门道，一股脑儿倾倒给斯派克——那位朋友在戏里挂头牌。我进来的时候跟您说过，我方才在同德利弗勋爵说话。他告诉我的正是：就在遇见我之前不久，他在伦敦碰见过那位演戏的先生，一个姓米夫林的家伙——亚瑟·米夫林。他如今就在伦敦，为一出从美国漂洋过来的新戏排练。您瞧见这一条消息的分量了吧？这就是说，您要是疑心我这番话，只消找到米夫林——他那出戏在哪家剧院上演我忘了，可您一转眼就能打听出来——请他作个佐证。您满意了吗？」

麦克伊彻恩没有作声。一个钟头之前，为了坚持认定吉米心术不正，他会斗争到最后一道战壕；可最近这十分钟里的种种变故动摇了他。他感到心头的天平起了回潮，偏向吉米这一边。

「您听我说，麦克伊彻恩先生，」吉米说，「我盼着您安安静静听我讲上一两分钟。天底下实在没有一点理由，非叫咱们这样你掐我的喉咙、我掐你的喉咙不可。咱们尽可以交个朋友。握握手，这场架就算了。我猜您知道，我上这儿来见您是为了什么？」麦克伊彻恩没有说话。

===Original===
“You know that your daughter has broken off her engagement to Lord Dreever?”

“Then he was right!” said McEachern, half to himself. “It is you?”

Jimmy nodded. McEachern drummed his fingers on the table and stared thoughtfully at him.

“Is Molly—?” he said, at length. “Does Molly—?”

“Yes,” said Jimmy.

McEachern continued his drumming.

===Chinese===
「您知道您女儿已经同德利弗勋爵解除婚约了吗？」

「这么说他说得不错！」麦克伊彻恩半是自言自语。「是你？」

吉米点点头。麦克伊彻恩的手指在桌上敲鼓，若有所思地盯着他。

「莫莉她——？」他终于开口。「莫莉是不是——？」

「是的，」吉米说。

麦克伊彻恩继续着他的敲鼓。

===Original===
“Don’t think there’s been anything underhand about this,” said Jimmy. “She absolutely refused to do anything unless you gave your consent. She said you had been partners all her life, and she was going to do the square thing by you.”

“She did?” said McEachern eagerly.

“I think you ought to do the square thing by her. I’m not much, but she wants me. Do the square thing by her.”

McEachern was staring straight in front of him. There was a look in his eyes which Jimmy had never seen there before—a frightened, hunted look.

===Chinese===
「您别以为这里头有什么偷偷摸摸的事，」吉米说。「您不点头，她断然什么也不肯做。她说，您是她一辈子的搭伙人，她要光明磊落地待您。」

「她这么说？」麦克伊彻恩急切地问。

「我看您也该光明磊落地待她。我这个人不算什么，可她要的是我。光明磊落地待她吧。」

麦克伊彻恩直勾勾地望着正前方。他眼睛里有一种吉米从未见过的神色——一种惶恐的、走投无路的眼神。

===Original===
“It’s too late,” he burst out. “I’ll be square with her now, but it’s too late. I won’t stand in her way when I can make her happy. But I’ll lose her! Oh, my God, I’ll lose her!

“Did you think I had never said to myself,” he went on, “the things you said to me that day when we met here? Did you think I didn’t know what I was? Who should know it better than myself? But she didn’t—I’d kept it from her. I’d sweat for fear she would find out some day. When I came over here I thought I was safe; and then you came, and I saw you together. I thought you were a crook—you were with Mullins in New York—I told her you were a crook.”

“You told her that?”

“I said I knew it. I couldn’t tell her the truth why I thought so. I said I had made inquiries in New York and found out about you.”

===Chinese===
「太迟了，」他脱口喊道。「如今我愿意对她光明磊落，可太迟了。只要能让她幸福，我不会拦她的道。可我要失去她了！哦，天哪，我要失去她了！

「你当我就从来没有，」他接着说，「在心里对自己说过你那天在这儿会面上对我讲的那些话？你当我不知道自己是个什么东西？还有谁比我更清楚？可她不知道——我一直瞒着她。我日夜出冷汗，唯恐有一天叫她察觉得了。当初渡海到这边来，我以为从此安稳了；后来你来了，我看见你们俩在一起。我当你是条窃贼——在纽约你就同马林斯混在一处——我对她说你是个窃贼。」

「你对她这样说了？」

「我说我确知此事。我又不能把起疑的缘故照实告诉她。我说我在纽约打听过，把你的底细摸清了。」

===Original===
Jimmy saw now. The mystery was solved. So that was why Molly had allowed them to force her into the engagement with Dreever.

“I see,” he said slowly.

McEachern gripped the table in silence.

“I see,” said Jimmy again. “You mean she’ll want an explanation?”

He thought for a moment.

===Chinese===
吉米这下明白了。谜底解开了。莫莉原先正是因此，才任凭他们强按着同德利弗订了婚。

「我明白了，」他缓缓地说。

麦克伊彻恩默默抠住桌沿。

「我明白了，」吉米又说了一遍。「您是说，她会要个交代？」

他想了片刻。

===Original===
“You must tell her,” he said quickly. “For your own sake you must tell her. Go and do it now. Wake up, man!” He shook him by the shoulder. “Go and do it now. She’ll forgive you. Don’t be afraid of that. Go and look for her and tell her now.”

McEachern roused himself.

“I will,” he said.

“It’s the only way,” said Jimmy.

McEachern opened the door, then fell back a pace. Jimmy could hear voices in the passage outside. He recognised Lord Dreever’s.

===Chinese===
「您必须告诉她，」他赶紧说。「为您自己着想，您也必须告诉她。现在就去说。醒醒，老兄！」他抓住他的肩膀摇了摇。「现在就去说。她会原谅您的，别怕这个。现在就去找她，告诉她。」

麦克伊彻恩振作起来。

「我去，」他说。

「这是唯一的路子，」吉米说。

麦克伊彻恩开了门，随即倒退一步。吉米听得见外面走廊里的人声。他听出那是德利弗勋爵的嗓音。

===Original===
McEachern continued to back away from the door.

Lord Dreever entered, with Molly on his arm.

“Halloa!” said his lordship, looking round.

“Halloa, Pitt! Here we all are; what?”

“Lord Dreever wanted to smoke,” said Molly.

===Chinese===
麦克伊彻恩继续从门口步步后退。

德利弗勋爵走了进来，挽着莫莉。

「哟！」勋爵老爷环顾四周，说。

「哟，皮特！咱们这可全都在场啦；怎么样？」

「德利弗勋爵想抽口烟，」莫莉说。

===Original===
She smiled, but there was anxiety in her eyes. She looked quickly at her father and at Jimmy.

“Molly, my dear,” said McEachern huskily, “I want to speak to you for a moment.”

Jimmy took his lordship by the arm.

“Come along, Dreever,” he said. “You can come and sit out with me. We’ll go and smoke on the terrace.”

===Chinese===
她微笑着，眼睛里却含着不安。她飞快地望了望父亲，又望了望吉米。

「莫莉，我的孩子，」麦克伊彻恩哑着嗓子说，「我要跟你说句话。」

吉米挽住勋爵老爷的胳膊。

「来吧，德利弗，」他说。「你可以来陪我坐场子。咱们上露台抽烟去。」

===Original===
They left the room together.

“What does the old boy want?” inquired his lordship. “Are you and Miss McEachern—?”

“We are,” said Jimmy.

“By Jove! I say, old chap! Million congratulations and all that sort of rot, you know!”

===Chinese===
他们一道出了房间。

「老家伙要干什么？」勋爵老爷问。「您跟麦克伊彻恩小姐是不是——？」

「是的，」吉米说。

「哎呀老天！我说，老伙计！千百万个恭喜，还有那一套零碎儿，你知道！」

---

===Original===
His lordship had to resume his duties in the ballroom after a while; but Jimmy sat on, smoking and thinking.

In the general stillness the opening of the door at the top of the steps came sharply to his ears. He looked up. Two figures were silhouetted for a moment against the light, and then the door closed again. They began to move slowly down the steps.

Jimmy had recognised them. He got up. He was in the shadow; they could not see him. They began to walk down the terrace. They were quite close now. Neither was speaking, but presently, when they were but a few feet away, they stopped. There was the splutter of a match, and McEachern lit a cigar. In the yellow light his face was clearly visible. Jimmy looked, and was content.

===Chinese===
过了一阵，勋爵老爷不得不回舞厅去重理他的职守；吉米却坐着没动，抽着烟，想着心事。

在四下一片岑寂之中，台阶顶上那扇门开动的声响，清晰地传进他的耳朵。他抬起头。两个人影在灯光前映出侧影，一瞬之后，门又合上了。他们开始顺着台阶慢慢走下来。

吉米已经认出了他们。他站起身。他隐在阴影里；他们瞧不见他。两人沿着露台走来，如今已相距很近。谁也没有说话；走了几步，当他们相隔不过几英尺的时候，停住了。火柴嗤的一声擦亮了，麦克伊彻恩点起一支雪茄。黄澄澄的光里，他的脸看得清清楚楚。吉米看在眼里，心下安然。

## XXX Conclusion / 结局

===Original===
The American liner *St. Louis* lay in the Empress Dock at Southampton, taking aboard her passengers. All sorts and conditions of men flowed in an unceasing stream up the gangway.

Leaning over the second-class railing, Jimmy Pitt and Spike Mullins watched them thoughtfully.

“Well, Spike,” said Jimmy, “your schooner’s on the tide now, isn’t it? Your vessel’s at the quay. You’ve got some queer-looking fellow travellers. Don’t miss the two Cingalese sports and the man in the turban and baggy breeches. I wonder if they’re airtight? Useful if he fell overboard.”

===Chinese===
美国邮轮「圣路易斯号」停泊在南安普顿（Southampton）的皇后码头（Empress Dock），正在搭载乘客。三教九流、形形色色的人，汇成一股络绎不绝的人流，涌上舷梯。

吉米·皮特和斯派克·马林斯凭倚在二等舱的栏杆上，若有所思地望着他们。

「怎么样，斯派克，」吉米说，「你的帆船这会儿正赶上涨潮，是不是？你的船就泊在码头边。你这趟的同船旅伴里，有几位模样古怪的。可别错过那两位锡兰（Cingalese）来的玩家老爷，还有那位裹着头巾、穿灯笼裤的先生。真不知道那身行头是不是密不透水？他万一失足落水，倒还派得上用场。」

===Original===
“Sure,” said Spike, directing a contemplative eye towards the garment in question. “He knows his business.”

“I wonder what those men on the deck are writing? They’ve been scribbling away ever since we came here. Probably society journalists. We shall see in next week’s papers, ‘Among the second-class passengers we noticed Mr. “Spike” Mullins looking as cheery as ever.’ It’s a pity you’re so set on going, Spike. Why not change your mind and stop?”

For a moment Spike looked wistful. Then his countenance resumed its woodenness. “Dere ain’t no use for me dis side, boss,” he said. “New York’s de spot. Youse don’t want none of me now you’re married. How’s Miss Molly, boss?”

“Splendid, Spike, thanks. We’re going over to France by tonight’s boat.

===Chinese===
「那可不，」斯派克说着，若有所思地朝那件受议论的衣裳瞟了一眼。「这位先生懂得自己的行当。」

「不知道甲板上那几位先生在写什么？打从咱们上这儿来，他们就一直在涂涂写写。多半是采访上流社会的记者。下星期的报纸上，咱们准能看到这么一条：『在二等舱乘客中间，我们注意到「斯派克」·马林斯先生，还是那样满脸喜气。』你一门心思要走，真是可惜，斯派克。怎么不改了主意，留下来呢？」

斯派克一时间显得怅然若失。随即他的脸又恢复了木头木脑的神情。「这边儿用不着我这样的人，老大，」他说。「纽约才是我的地盘。您如今成了家，就不需要我这样的人了。莫莉小姐好吗，老大？」

「好极了，多谢，斯派克。我们今晚就搭船去法国。」

===Original===
“It’s been a queer business,” said Jimmy, after a pause—“a deuced queer business. Still, I’ve come very well out of it, at any rate. It seems to me that you’re the only one of us who doesn’t end happily, Spike. I’m married. McEachern’s butted into society so deep that it would take an excavating party with dynamite to get him out of it. Molly—well, Molly’s made a bad bargain, but I hope she won’t regret it. We’re all going some, except you. You’re going out on the old trail again—which begins in Third Avenue and ends in Sing Sing. Why tear yourself away, Spike?”

Spike concentrated his gaze on a weedy young emigrant in a blue jersey, who was having his eye examined by the overworked doctor, and seemed to be resenting it.

“Dere’s nuttin’ doin’ dis side, boss,” he said at length. “I want to get busy.”

“Ulysses Mullins!” said Jimmy, looking at him curiously. “I know the feeling. There’s only one cure. I sketched it out for you once, but I doubt if you’ll ever take it. You don’t think a lot of women, do you? You’re the rugged bachelor.”

===Chinese===
「这一整桩事真叫古怪，」吉米停了停，说道。「古怪得见鬼。话虽如此，无论如何，我自己在这桩事里收场收得很不坏。要我说，咱们这些人里，唯独你一个落不着个好结局，斯派克。我结了婚。麦克伊彻恩一头扎进了上流社会，扎得那么深，得派一支背着炸药的挖掘队，才能把他掏出来。莫莉嘛——莫莉是做了一笔吃亏的买卖，但愿她日后不会后悔。咱们个个都要发达了，唯独你除外。你又要踏上老道上路的——那条道起自第三大道，终点站是辛辛监狱（Sing Sing）。何苦非要抽身走人呢，斯派克？」

斯派克把目光集中在一个瘦骨伶仃的年轻移民身上。那人穿着蓝色毛线衫，正让忙得不可开交的医生检查眼睛，一副老大不情愿的样子。

「这边儿没什么搞头，老大，」他终于开口。「我想忙活起来。」

「尤利西斯·马林斯！」吉米说，好奇地打量着他。「这种滋味我懂。治它的药方只有一张。我从前给你勾画过一回，不过我疑心你多半不会采用。你对女人不怎么看重，对吧？你是个铁杆的独身汉。」

===Original===
“Goils—!” began Spike comprehensively, and abandoned the topic without dilating on it further.

“Dose were great jools, boss,” said Spike thoughtfully.

“I believe you’re still brooding over them, Spike.”

“We could have got away wit dem, if you’d have stood for it—dead easy.”

“You are brooding over them. Spike, I’ll tell you something which will console you a little before you start out on your wanderings. It’s in confidence, so keep it dark. That necklace was paste.”

===Chinese===
「姑娘们——！」斯派克一开口就想一言以蔽之，随即撇下了这个话头，没有再铺陈下去。

「那可真是批上好的珠宝，老大，」斯派克若有所思地说。

「我看你到现在还惦记着它们呢，斯派克。」

「当初您要是肯点头，咱们本可以把它们弄到手的——容易得要死。」

「你这就是还惦记着呢。斯派克，趁你还没动身去浪迹江湖，我讲件事给你听，多少能宽慰宽慰你。这话是私房话，你得守口如瓶。那条项链是玻璃钻的。」

===Original===
“What’s dat?”

“Nothing but paste. I spotted it directly you handed them to me. It wasn’t worth a hundred dollars.”

A light of understanding came into Spike’s eyes. His face beamed with the smile of one to whom dark matters are made clear.

“So dat’s why you wouldn’t stand for getting away wit it!” he exclaimed.

===Chinese===
「您说啥？」

「除了玻璃钻什么都不是。你把它们递到我手里的当儿，我就瞧出来了。它连一百美元都不值。」

斯派克的眼里透出恍然大悟的光。他满脸绽开笑容，喜气洋洋——那是闷葫芦终于被人解开的人才有的笑容。

「所以您才不肯由着咱们把它弄走啊！」他大声说。

---

===Original===
The last voyager had embarked. The deck was full to congestion.

“They’ll be sending us ashore in a minute,” said Jimmy. “I’d better be moving. Let me know how you’re getting on, Spike, from time to time. You know the address. And, I say, it’s just possible you may find you want a dollar or two every now and then—when you’re going to buy another aeroplane, for instance. Well, you know where to write to for it, don’t you?”

“T’anks, boss. But dat’ll be all right. I’m goin’ to sit in at anodder game dis time—politics, boss. A fr’en’ of a mug what I knows has gotten a pull. He’ll find me a job.”

“Politics!” said Jimmy. “I never thought of that. ‘My brother Dan is an alderman with a grip on the Seventh Ward!’” he quoted softly. “Why, you’ll be a boss before you know where you are.”

===Chinese===
最后一位旅客也登了船。甲板拥挤到了极点。

「他们马上就要把咱们赶上岸了，」吉米说。「我该挪窝了。有空常来信说说你的近况，斯派克。你知道地址。哦对了，说不定你也会发觉自己时不时缺个一块两块——比方说，要再买一架飞机的时候。这个嘛，你知道往哪儿写信去要，对吧？」

「多谢，老大。不过那倒不必。这回我打算入另一场的伙——政治这一行，老大。我认得一个家伙，他有个朋友很吃得开。那位朋友会给我寻份差事。」

「政治！」吉米说。「这我可没想到。『我哥哥丹是位市议员，整个第七选区都攥在他手心里！』」他轻声念叨了一句。「嘿，转眼之间，你自己就要当上『老大』了。」

===Original===
“Sure,” said Spike, grinning modestly.

“You ought to be a thundering success in American politics,” said Jimmy. “You’ve got all the necessary qualities.”

A steward passed.

“Any more for the shore?”

===Chinese===
「那可不，」斯派克谦虚地咧嘴一笑。

「你搞美国的政治，准能大获成功，」吉米说。「该有的素质，你一样不缺。」

一名乘务员走了过去。

「还有要上岸的吗？」

===Original===
“Well, Spike—” said Jimmy.

“Goodbye, boss.”

“Goodbye,” said Jimmy. “And good luck.”

===Chinese===
「那么，斯派克——」吉米说。

「再见，老大。」

「再见，」吉米说。「祝你好运。」

---

===Original===
The sun had gone behind the clouds. As the ship slid out on its way a stray beam pierced the greyness.

It shone on a red head.

===Chinese===
太阳隐到了云层背后。轮船滑出泊位、踏上航程时，一缕游散的光束刺破了满天的铅灰。

它正照在一头红发上。
